---------- turn 16 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, F32.0, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thanks. I guess what finally made me make the appointment is I’ve been forgetting these little day-to-day things that I normally would handle, or at least I feel like I should be handling. Like I’ll mean to return a phone call and then realize later I never did, or I’ll have a registration form that needs to be turned in and somehow it just sits there, even though I know it’s important. Or I’ll plan to stop somewhere on the way home for an errand, and then I get home and it hits me that I completely forgot.\n\nIt’s not one huge thing, exactly, but it’s been happening enough that it’s starting to make me feel embarrassed and frustrated with myself. I work as a secretary, so keeping track of calls and forms and little details is kind of part of who I’m supposed to be, you know? And lately it feels like those small things keep slipping through.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, and sometimes I feel kind of embarrassed when I realize I dropped the ball on something simple. But “sad or down” like… low mood all day, or crying, or feeling hopeless? I don’t think that’s really what’s been going on.\n\nIt’s more like I’m annoyed and a little worried because I keep missing things I feel like I should be on top of. So emotionally it doesn’t feel great, but I haven’t noticed myself being especially sad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I mean, I don’t think so in the way I usually think of anxiety. I’m not walking around panicky or feeling like something terrible is about to happen. I do get tense with myself when I realize I forgot something, or if I’m worried someone is going to notice I missed a detail, but that feels more like being frustrated or embarrassed than anxiety, I guess.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure where the line is. Like, if I have a pile of things I meant to do and I suddenly remember one of them, I can feel this little rush of “oh no,” but it passes once I deal with it—or once I tell myself I’ll deal with it, even if that doesn’t always happen right away. But lately I wouldn’t say I’ve been generally anxious all the time, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually a big part of it. It’s not that I can never concentrate, because sometimes I’m fine if it’s quiet and I’m really settled into something. But at work especially, it doesn’t take much to pull me away from what I’m doing.\n\nLike, I’ll be in the middle of entering something or working through a form, and then the phone rings—maybe not even my phone, just one nearby—or people start talking at the next desk, and suddenly my attention is just gone. I’ll kind of tune into that without meaning to, and then when I look back at what I was doing I have to figure out where I left off. Sometimes it’s even more annoying because it’s not anything happening around me, it’s just some random thought popping in, like remembering something at home or thinking, “Oh, I need to check on that later,” and then I’m mentally off in a completely different direction.\n\nIt’s frustrating because the tasks themselves aren’t always hard. It’s more like staying with them is hard when there’s anything else going on around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I still like the things I normally like. If I have time to watch something I enjoy or talk to a friend or do something relaxing, I do still enjoy it once I’m doing it.\n\nSometimes I don’t get around to things I meant to do for myself, but it doesn’t feel like I’ve lost interest exactly. It’s more like the day gets away from me, or I put it off, and then suddenly it’s late and I’m tired. But the interest is still there, I think. So no, I haven’t really noticed that I’m not enjoying things anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these problems with forgetting things and having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: It’s been… noticeably worse for about 15 months, I’d say. Around when I started this evening certificate program. I mean, I had little things before, like being scattered sometimes, but I could usually sort of cover for it or make it work with my normal routine.\n\nBut once I added classes in the evenings on top of work, it felt like everything got harder to keep track of. There were more deadlines, more papers, more things I had to remember after already working all day, and that’s when the forgetting and losing focus started feeling like a real problem instead of just an annoying habit.\n\nSo, not brand new, exactly, but the point where I really started noticing it and feeling like I couldn’t manage it as well was about 15 months ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, I don’t weigh myself very regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number or anything like that, but my clothes seem to fit about the same, and nobody’s mentioned that I look like I’ve lost or gained a lot of weight.\n\nThere might be normal little ups and downs, like if I’m busy or eating differently for a few days, but nothing I’d call significant or sudden. So I don’t think there’s been a major change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything lately?\n\nPatient: Guilty… um, not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I have the normal kind of guilt, like if I forget to call someone back or I put something off longer than I meant to, I’ll feel bad about it for a bit. But I don’t think I’ve been sitting around feeling guilty in a big, heavy way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess I can be hard on myself sometimes, like thinking, “Why didn’t I just do that earlier?” or “I should be more on top of things,” but it feels more like frustration with myself than guilt about something specific. There isn’t one thing I’ve done that I’m constantly blaming myself for, and I’m not feeling guilty all day or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetting things and concentrating affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, not in a huge dramatic way where people are angry at me or relationships are falling apart, but it does spill over.\n\nWith family or friends, it’s more like I’ll forget to text someone back, or I’ll mean to follow up about plans and then realize days later that I never answered. Sometimes I can tell people are a little annoyed, and then I feel embarrassed because it looks like I don’t care, when really I just lost track of it.\n\nHonestly, the bigger impact has been with work and school. I’m still getting by, like I haven’t completely dropped the ball, but I’ve missed small follow-ups at work and a few school deadlines often enough that it worries me. I’m trying to move toward more responsibility, like eventually an Office Manager type role, and I worry that if people see me as unreliable or scattered, it could hurt my reputation or make them think I’m not ready for that.\n\nSo relationships with friends and family are affected some, but the thing that weighs on me more is how it might affect how people see me professionally and at school.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less pleasure than usual when doing things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy when I actually sit down and do them. Like if I’m watching a show I like, or talking with a friend, or doing something relaxing, I can still get into it and feel good.\n\nSometimes I might put things off or not get around to them, but it doesn’t feel like I’ve stopped caring about them or like they don’t give me any enjoyment anymore. It’s more just… I don’t know, life gets busy and I get distracted, and then suddenly the evening’s gone. But when I do the thing, I still like it.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I’ve felt less pleasure than usual. At least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in doing things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve been less interested, no. At least, not in the way I think you mean, like losing interest in things or not wanting to do them anymore.\n\nIt’s more that I sometimes don’t get around to doing things I like, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think, “Oh, I should watch that show,” or “I should call my friend,” or “I want to do something relaxing tonight,” and then somehow the time just goes by and I didn’t actually do it. But the interest is still there. If someone suggested it, or if I actually started doing it, I’d usually be into it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped wanting to do enjoyable things. It’s more like following through or making space for them can be hit or miss.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make significant changes to your daily routine or rely on others more often because of your forgetfulness and difficulty concentrating?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve made huge changes, like rearranged my whole life around it or anything. I’m still functioning day to day, and I’m getting by. But I do feel like I’m having to compensate more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work, for example, it’s not that I’m completely missing major things all the time, but I’ve missed smaller follow-ups enough that it makes me nervous. Like something I meant to circle back on, or a little detail that needed a response, and then later I’m thinking, “How did I let that slip?” And with school, I’ve had a few deadlines where I either cut it too close or missed the timing, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nI don’t rely on other people constantly, but I do feel like I’m starting to need reminders or extra checking in more than I want to. And the part that really bothers me is that I’m trying to show I can handle more responsibility. I’d eventually like to move toward something like an Office Manager role, and I worry that if these little missed things keep happening, people are going to see me as scattered or unreliable instead of capable. So… not a major life overhaul, but yes, it’s affecting how I manage things and how much I worry about staying on top of everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes when you're doing something, like not noticing details or not following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how often is “often,” I guess. I do make mistakes sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call them careless exactly. Like, I’m usually trying to pay attention, and I don’t feel like I’m being sloppy on purpose or rushing through everything like I don’t care.\n\nThere are definitely moments where I’ll look back and realize I missed a small detail, or I thought I understood what someone wanted and then later it turns out I didn’t catch one part of it. But I don’t know if that’s more than what other people do. It’s hard for me to judge, because at work especially I try to double-check things, and I can get kind of anxious about making sure I didn’t miss anything.\n\nSo… yes, it happens sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d say it’s constant or extreme. It’s more like little things that make me think, “Ugh, why didn’t I notice that?” rather than huge mistakes all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say “unusually” tired. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially after a long day, and sometimes I’ll feel kind of drained, but I haven’t noticed it as this major new thing where I’m exhausted all the time or can’t get through the day.\n\nIt’s more like… if I’m tired, I usually assume it’s because the day was busy or I stayed up too late or just didn’t manage my time well. I don’t think I’ve been sitting there thinking, “Wow, something is really wrong, I’m abnormally fatigued.” So I guess my answer is no, not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to significantly adjust your work or school schedule because of your difficulties with forgetting things and concentrating?\n\nPatient: Not significantly, no. I haven’t, like, changed my work hours or dropped school or anything like that. I’m still getting by and keeping things mostly on track.\n\nBut I do feel like I’m having to manage around it more than I used to. At work, it’s more the small follow-ups that get me — like an email I meant to answer, or something I was supposed to check back on, and then it slips until later. And with school, there have been deadlines where I cut it way too close or didn’t realize the timing until it was almost too late.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say my schedule has been formally adjusted, but I’m definitely trying to build in extra time or reminders because I’m worried about how it looks. I want to be seen as dependable, especially because I’d like to move toward an Office Manager kind of role eventually, and I worry these little missed things could make people think I’m not ready for that.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with forgetting things and concentrating something that you experience every day?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, in some form it’s pretty much every day, but it’s not like every day is a disaster or I’m forgetting major things constantly. It’s more these little everyday things that I normally would expect myself to just handle.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to return a call, and then the day gets away from me and I realize later I never did it. Or there’ll be a form I need to turn in — like a registration form or something simple — and it just sits there because I keep thinking I’ll do it in a minute, and then suddenly it’s later than I thought. Or I’ll tell myself I need to stop somewhere on the way home for an errand, and then I drive right past it or get home and remember.\n\nThat’s kind of what finally made me feel like I should ask for help. It’s not one huge thing, it’s that these little things keep slipping, and they’re things I feel like I should be able to keep track of. So yes, I’d say it shows up most days, even if the exact thing changes from day to day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s hard for me to judge, because at work especially I try to double-check things, and I can get kind of anxious about making sure I didn’t miss anything.\n\nSo… yes, it happens sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d say it’s constant or extreme. It’s more like little things that make me think, “Ugh, why didn’t I notice that?” rather than huge mistakes all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say “unusually” tired. It’s more like little things that make me think, “Ugh, why didn’t I notice that?” rather than huge mistakes all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say “unusually” tired. So yes, I’d say it shows up most days, even if the exact thing changes from day to day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two weeks or more where you felt sad or depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve definitely had days where I felt down or discouraged, especially when I’m overwhelmed or feel like I’m not keeping up, but I don’t remember a stretch of two weeks or more where I was sad or depressed nearly every day.\n\nI guess I’d say my mood can dip here and there, but it doesn’t feel like it stays that way continuously. It’s more tied to specific things happening, and then it passes. So no, not that I can think of.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in how your friends or family interact with you since you started experiencing these difficulties with forgetting things and concentrating?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 36 / secretary_or_administrative_assistant / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’ve noticed a big change, honestly. I don’t think anyone has sat me down or anything like, “You’re different,” or “What’s going on with you?” It’s more… if anything, I sometimes wonder if they’re getting a little used to reminding me about things, but I don’t know if that’s actually changed or if I’m just more aware of it now.

Like with family, someone might repeat something or check, “Did you remember to do that?” but I can’t really say it’s new-new. And with friends, I don’t think they treat me differently. I haven’t had anyone pull away or seem upset in a major way. I might apologize more if I’m late responding or if I forget to follow up, but I don’t know that their interaction with me has really changed.

So… no, not in any obvious way that I can point to. It’s more my own worry that people might start seeing me as flaky, but I don’t know that they actually are.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, F32.0, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thanks. I guess what finally made me make the appointment is I’ve been forgetting these little day-to-day things that I normally would handle, or at least I feel like I should be handling. Like I’ll mean to return a phone call and then realize later I never did, or I’ll have a registration form that needs to be turned in and somehow it just sits there, even though I know it’s important. Or I’ll plan to stop somewhere on the way home for an errand, and then I get home and it hits me that I completely forgot.\n\nIt’s not one huge thing, exactly, but it’s been happening enough that it’s starting to make me feel embarrassed and frustrated with myself. I work as a secretary, so keeping track of calls and forms and little details is kind of part of who I’m supposed to be, you know? And lately it feels like those small things keep slipping through.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, and sometimes I feel kind of embarrassed when I realize I dropped the ball on something simple. But “sad or down” like… low mood all day, or crying, or feeling hopeless? I don’t think that’s really what’s been going on.\n\nIt’s more like I’m annoyed and a little worried because I keep missing things I feel like I should be on top of. So emotionally it doesn’t feel great, but I haven’t noticed myself being especially sad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I mean, I don’t think so in the way I usually think of anxiety. I’m not walking around panicky or feeling like something terrible is about to happen. I do get tense with myself when I realize I forgot something, or if I’m worried someone is going to notice I missed a detail, but that feels more like being frustrated or embarrassed than anxiety, I guess.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure where the line is. Like, if I have a pile of things I meant to do and I suddenly remember one of them, I can feel this little rush of “oh no,” but it passes once I deal with it—or once I tell myself I’ll deal with it, even if that doesn’t always happen right away. But lately I wouldn’t say I’ve been generally anxious all the time, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually a big part of it. It’s not that I can never concentrate, because sometimes I’m fine if it’s quiet and I’m really settled into something. But at work especially, it doesn’t take much to pull me away from what I’m doing.\n\nLike, I’ll be in the middle of entering something or working through a form, and then the phone rings—maybe not even my phone, just one nearby—or people start talking at the next desk, and suddenly my attention is just gone. I’ll kind of tune into that without meaning to, and then when I look back at what I was doing I have to figure out where I left off. Sometimes it’s even more annoying because it’s not anything happening around me, it’s just some random thought popping in, like remembering something at home or thinking, “Oh, I need to check on that later,” and then I’m mentally off in a completely different direction.\n\nIt’s frustrating because the tasks themselves aren’t always hard. It’s more like staying with them is hard when there’s anything else going on around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I still like the things I normally like. If I have time to watch something I enjoy or talk to a friend or do something relaxing, I do still enjoy it once I’m doing it.\n\nSometimes I don’t get around to things I meant to do for myself, but it doesn’t feel like I’ve lost interest exactly. It’s more like the day gets away from me, or I put it off, and then suddenly it’s late and I’m tired. But the interest is still there, I think. So no, I haven’t really noticed that I’m not enjoying things anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these problems with forgetting things and having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: It’s been… noticeably worse for about 15 months, I’d say. Around when I started this evening certificate program. I mean, I had little things before, like being scattered sometimes, but I could usually sort of cover for it or make it work with my normal routine.\n\nBut once I added classes in the evenings on top of work, it felt like everything got harder to keep track of. There were more deadlines, more papers, more things I had to remember after already working all day, and that’s when the forgetting and losing focus started feeling like a real problem instead of just an annoying habit.\n\nSo, not brand new, exactly, but the point where I really started noticing it and feeling like I couldn’t manage it as well was about 15 months ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, I don’t weigh myself very regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number or anything like that, but my clothes seem to fit about the same, and nobody’s mentioned that I look like I’ve lost or gained a lot of weight.\n\nThere might be normal little ups and downs, like if I’m busy or eating differently for a few days, but nothing I’d call significant or sudden. So I don’t think there’s been a major change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything lately?\n\nPatient: Guilty… um, not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I have the normal kind of guilt, like if I forget to call someone back or I put something off longer than I meant to, I’ll feel bad about it for a bit. But I don’t think I’ve been sitting around feeling guilty in a big, heavy way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess I can be hard on myself sometimes, like thinking, “Why didn’t I just do that earlier?” or “I should be more on top of things,” but it feels more like frustration with myself than guilt about something specific. There isn’t one thing I’ve done that I’m constantly blaming myself for, and I’m not feeling guilty all day or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetting things and concentrating affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, not in a huge dramatic way where people are angry at me or relationships are falling apart, but it does spill over.\n\nWith family or friends, it’s more like I’ll forget to text someone back, or I’ll mean to follow up about plans and then realize days later that I never answered. Sometimes I can tell people are a little annoyed, and then I feel embarrassed because it looks like I don’t care, when really I just lost track of it.\n\nHonestly, the bigger impact has been with work and school. I’m still getting by, like I haven’t completely dropped the ball, but I’ve missed small follow-ups at work and a few school deadlines often enough that it worries me. I’m trying to move toward more responsibility, like eventually an Office Manager type role, and I worry that if people see me as unreliable or scattered, it could hurt my reputation or make them think I’m not ready for that.\n\nSo relationships with friends and family are affected some, but the thing that weighs on me more is how it might affect how people see me professionally and at school.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less pleasure than usual when doing things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy when I actually sit down and do them. Like if I’m watching a show I like, or talking with a friend, or doing something relaxing, I can still get into it and feel good.\n\nSometimes I might put things off or not get around to them, but it doesn’t feel like I’ve stopped caring about them or like they don’t give me any enjoyment anymore. It’s more just… I don’t know, life gets busy and I get distracted, and then suddenly the evening’s gone. But when I do the thing, I still like it.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I’ve felt less pleasure than usual. At least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in doing things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve been less interested, no. At least, not in the way I think you mean, like losing interest in things or not wanting to do them anymore.\n\nIt’s more that I sometimes don’t get around to doing things I like, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think, “Oh, I should watch that show,” or “I should call my friend,” or “I want to do something relaxing tonight,” and then somehow the time just goes by and I didn’t actually do it. But the interest is still there. If someone suggested it, or if I actually started doing it, I’d usually be into it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped wanting to do enjoyable things. It’s more like following through or making space for them can be hit or miss.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make significant changes to your daily routine or rely on others more often because of your forgetfulness and difficulty concentrating?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve made huge changes, like rearranged my whole life around it or anything. I’m still functioning day to day, and I’m getting by. But I do feel like I’m having to compensate more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work, for example, it’s not that I’m completely missing major things all the time, but I’ve missed smaller follow-ups enough that it makes me nervous. Like something I meant to circle back on, or a little detail that needed a response, and then later I’m thinking, “How did I let that slip?” And with school, I’ve had a few deadlines where I either cut it too close or missed the timing, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nI don’t rely on other people constantly, but I do feel like I’m starting to need reminders or extra checking in more than I want to. And the part that really bothers me is that I’m trying to show I can handle more responsibility. I’d eventually like to move toward something like an Office Manager role, and I worry that if these little missed things keep happening, people are going to see me as scattered or unreliable instead of capable. So… not a major life overhaul, but yes, it’s affecting how I manage things and how much I worry about staying on top of everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes when you're doing something, like not noticing details or not following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how often is “often,” I guess. I do make mistakes sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call them careless exactly. Like, I’m usually trying to pay attention, and I don’t feel like I’m being sloppy on purpose or rushing through everything like I don’t care.\n\nThere are definitely moments where I’ll look back and realize I missed a small detail, or I thought I understood what someone wanted and then later it turns out I didn’t catch one part of it. But I don’t know if that’s more than what other people do. It’s hard for me to judge, because at work especially I try to double-check things, and I can get kind of anxious about making sure I didn’t miss anything.\n\nSo… yes, it happens sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d say it’s constant or extreme. It’s more like little things that make me think, “Ugh, why didn’t I notice that?” rather than huge mistakes all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say “unusually” tired. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially after a long day, and sometimes I’ll feel kind of drained, but I haven’t noticed it as this major new thing where I’m exhausted all the time or can’t get through the day.\n\nIt’s more like… if I’m tired, I usually assume it’s because the day was busy or I stayed up too late or just didn’t manage my time well. I don’t think I’ve been sitting there thinking, “Wow, something is really wrong, I’m abnormally fatigued.” So I guess my answer is no, not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to significantly adjust your work or school schedule because of your difficulties with forgetting things and concentrating?\n\nPatient: Not significantly, no. I haven’t, like, changed my work hours or dropped school or anything like that. I’m still getting by and keeping things mostly on track.\n\nBut I do feel like I’m having to manage around it more than I used to. At work, it’s more the small follow-ups that get me — like an email I meant to answer, or something I was supposed to check back on, and then it slips until later. And with school, there have been deadlines where I cut it way too close or didn’t realize the timing until it was almost too late.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say my schedule has been formally adjusted, but I’m definitely trying to build in extra time or reminders because I’m worried about how it looks. I want to be seen as dependable, especially because I’d like to move toward an Office Manager kind of role eventually, and I worry these little missed things could make people think I’m not ready for that.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with forgetting things and concentrating something that you experience every day?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, in some form it’s pretty much every day, but it’s not like every day is a disaster or I’m forgetting major things constantly. It’s more these little everyday things that I normally would expect myself to just handle.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to return a call, and then the day gets away from me and I realize later I never did it. Or there’ll be a form I need to turn in — like a registration form or something simple — and it just sits there because I keep thinking I’ll do it in a minute, and then suddenly it’s later than I thought. Or I’ll tell myself I need to stop somewhere on the way home for an errand, and then I drive right past it or get home and remember.\n\nThat’s kind of what finally made me feel like I should ask for help. It’s not one huge thing, it’s that these little things keep slipping, and they’re things I feel like I should be able to keep track of. So yes, I’d say it shows up most days, even if the exact thing changes from day to day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two weeks or more where you felt sad or depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve definitely had days where I felt down or discouraged, especially when I’m overwhelmed or feel like I’m not keeping up, but I don’t remember a stretch of two weeks or more where I was sad or depressed nearly every day.\n\nI guess I’d say my mood can dip here and there, but it doesn’t feel like it stays that way continuously. It’s more like following through or making space for them can be hit or miss.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make significant changes to your daily routine or rely on others more often because of your forgetfulness and difficulty concentrating?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve made huge changes, like rearranged my whole life around it or anything. I’m still functioning day to day, and I’m getting by. But I do feel like I’m having to compensate more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work, for example, it’s not that I’m completely missing major things all the time, but I’ve missed smaller follow-ups enough that it makes me nervous. Like something I meant to circle back on, or a little detail that needed a response, and then later I’m thinking, “How did I let that slip?” And with school, I’ve had a few deadlines where I either cut it too close or missed the timing, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nI don’t rely on other people constantly, but I do feel like I’m starting to need reminders or extra checking in more than I want to. And the part that really bothers me is that I’m trying to show I can handle more responsibility. I’d eventually like to move toward something like an Office Manager role, and I worry that if these little missed things keep happening, people are going to see me as scattered or unreliable instead of capable. So… not a major life overhaul, but yes, it’s affecting how I manage things and how much I worry about staying on top of everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes when you're doing something, like not noticing details or not following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how often is “often,” I guess. I do make mistakes sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call them careless exactly. Like, I’m usually trying to pay attention, and I don’t feel like I’m being sloppy on purpose or rushing through everything like I don’t care.\n\nThere are definitely moments where I’ll look back and realize I missed a small detail, or I thought I understood what someone wanted and then later it turns out I didn’t catch one part of it. But I don’t know if that’s more than what other people do. It’s hard for me to judge, because at work especially I try to double-check things, and I can get kind of anxious about making sure I didn’t miss anything.\n\nSo… yes, it happens sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d say it’s constant or extreme. It’s more like little things that make me think, “Ugh, why didn’t I notice that?” rather than huge mistakes all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say “unusually” tired. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially after a long day, and sometimes I’ll feel kind of drained, but I haven’t noticed it as this major new thing where I’m exhausted all the time or can’t get through the day.\n\nIt’s more like… if I’m tired, I usually assume it’s because the day was busy or I stayed up too late or just didn’t manage my time well. I don’t think I’ve been sitting there thinking, “Wow, something is really wrong, I’m abnormally fatigued.” So I guess my answer is no, not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to significantly adjust your work or school schedule because of your difficulties with forgetting things and concentrating?\n\nPatient: Not significantly, no. I haven’t, like, changed my work hours or dropped school or anything like that. I’m still getting by and keeping things mostly on track.\n\nBut I do feel like I’m having to manage around it more than I used to. At work, it’s more the small follow-ups that get me — like an email I meant to answer, or something I was supposed to check back on, and then it slips until later. And with school, there have been deadlines where I cut it way too close or didn’t realize the timing until it was almost too late.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say my schedule has been formally adjusted, but I’m definitely trying to build in extra time or reminders because I’m worried about how it looks. I want to be seen as dependable, especially because I’d like to move toward an Office Manager kind of role eventually, and I worry these little missed things could make people think I’m not ready for that.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with forgetting things and concentrating something that you experience every day?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, in some form it’s pretty much every day, but it’s not like every day is a disaster or I’m forgetting major things constantly. It’s more these little everyday things that I normally would expect myself to just handle.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to return a call, and then the day gets away from me and I realize later I never did it. Or there’ll be a form I need to turn in — like a registration form or something simple — and it just sits there because I keep thinking I’ll do it in a minute, and then suddenly it’s later than I thought. Or I’ll tell myself I need to stop somewhere on the way home for an errand, and then I drive right past it or get home and remember.\n\nThat’s kind of what finally made me feel like I should ask for help. It’s not one huge thing, it’s that these little things keep slipping, and they’re things I feel like I should be able to keep track of. So yes, I’d say it shows up most days, even if the exact thing changes from day to day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two weeks or more where you felt sad or depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve definitely had days where I felt down or discouraged, especially when I’m overwhelmed or feel like I’m not keeping up, but I don’t remember a stretch of two weeks or more where I was sad or depressed nearly every day.\n\nI guess I’d say my mood can dip here and there, but it doesn’t feel like it stays that way continuously. It’s more tied to specific things happening, and then it passes. I might apologize more if I’m late responding or if I forget to follow up, but I don’t know that their interaction with me has really changed.\n\nSo… no, not in any obvious way that I can point to. It’s more my own worry that people might start seeing me as flaky, but I don’t know that they actually are.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. More than two years? I don’t think I’d say it’s been noticeably like this for that long, no.

I mean, I’ve probably always had moments where I’d forget something or get distracted, like anyone does, but the point where it started feeling harder to manage was when I started my evening certificate program. That was about… 15 months ago, I think. Since then it’s been much more obvious because I’m trying to keep track of work during the day and then classes and assignments and forms and schedules in the evening, and that’s when I started feeling like, okay, this is becoming a pattern I’m not keeping up with very well.

So I wouldn’t say more than two years. I’d say about a year and a few months that it’s really been noticeable and harder to handle.

There isn’t one thing I’ve done that I’m constantly blaming myself for, and I’m not feeling guilty all day or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetting things and concentrating affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, not in a huge dramatic way where people are angry at me or relationships are falling apart, but it does spill over.\n\nWith family or friends, it’s more like I’ll forget to text someone back, or I’ll mean to follow up about plans and then realize days later that I never answered. Sometimes I can tell people are a little annoyed, and then I feel embarrassed because it looks like I don’t care, when really I just lost track of it.\n\nHonestly, the bigger impact has been with work and school. I’m still getting by, like I haven’t completely dropped the ball, but I’ve missed small follow-ups at work and a few school deadlines often enough that it worries me. I’m trying to move toward more responsibility, like eventually an Office Manager type role, and I worry that if people see me as unreliable or scattered, it could hurt my reputation or make them think I’m not ready for that.\n\nSo relationships with friends and family are affected some, but the thing that weighs on me more is how it might affect how people see me professionally and at school.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less pleasure than usual when doing things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy when I actually sit down and do them. Like if I’m watching a show I like, or talking with a friend, or doing something relaxing, I can still get into it and feel good.\n\nSometimes I might put things off or not get around to them, but it doesn’t feel like I’ve stopped caring about them or like they don’t give me any enjoyment anymore. It’s more just… I don’t know, life gets busy and I get distracted, and then suddenly the evening’s gone. But when I do the thing, I still like it.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I’ve felt less pleasure than usual. At least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in doing things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve been less interested, no. At least, not in the way I think you mean, like losing interest in things or not wanting to do them anymore.\n\nIt’s more that I sometimes don’t get around to doing things I like, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think, “Oh, I should watch that show,” or “I should call my friend,” or “I want to do something relaxing tonight,” and then somehow the time just goes by and I didn’t actually do it. But the interest is still there. If someone suggested it, or if I actually started doing it, I’d usually be into it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped wanting to do enjoyable things. It’s more like following through or making space for them can be hit or miss.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make significant changes to your daily routine or rely on others more often because of your forgetfulness and difficulty concentrating?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve made huge changes, like rearranged my whole life around it or anything. I’m still functioning day to day, and I’m getting by. But I do feel like I’m having to compensate more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work, for example, it’s not that I’m completely missing major things all the time, but I’ve missed smaller follow-ups enough that it makes me nervous. Like something I meant to circle back on, or a little detail that needed a response, and then later I’m thinking, “How did I let that slip?” And with school, I’ve had a few deadlines where I either cut it too close or missed the timing, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nI don’t rely on other people constantly, but I do feel like I’m starting to need reminders or extra checking in more than I want to. And the part that really bothers me is that I’m trying to show I can handle more responsibility. I’d eventually like to move toward something like an Office Manager role, and I worry that if these little missed things keep happening, people are going to see me as scattered or unreliable instead of capable. So… not a major life overhaul, but yes, it’s affecting how I manage things and how much I worry about staying on top of everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes when you're doing something, like not noticing details or not following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how often is “often,” I guess. I do make mistakes sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call them careless exactly. Like, I’m usually trying to pay attention, and I don’t feel like I’m being sloppy on purpose or rushing through everything like I don’t care.\n\nThere are definitely moments where I’ll look back and realize I missed a small detail, or I thought I understood what someone wanted and then later it turns out I didn’t catch one part of it. But I don’t know if that’s more than what other people do. It’s hard for me to judge, because at work especially I try to double-check things, and I can get kind of anxious about making sure I didn’t miss anything.\n\nSo… yes, it happens sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d say it’s constant or extreme. It’s more like little things that make me think, “Ugh, why didn’t I notice that?” rather than huge mistakes all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say “unusually” tired. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially after a long day, and sometimes I’ll feel kind of drained, but I haven’t noticed it as this major new thing where I’m exhausted all the time or can’t get through the day.\n\nIt’s more like… if I’m tired, I usually assume it’s because the day was busy or I stayed up too late or just didn’t manage my time well. I don’t think I’ve been sitting there thinking, “Wow, something is really wrong, I’m abnormally fatigued.” So I guess my answer is no, not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to significantly adjust your work or school schedule because of your difficulties with forgetting things and concentrating?\n\nPatient: Not significantly, no. I haven’t, like, changed my work hours or dropped school or anything like that. I’m still getting by and keeping things mostly on track.\n\nBut I do feel like I’m having to manage around it more than I used to. At work, it’s more the small follow-ups that get me — like an email I meant to answer, or something I was supposed to check back on, and then it slips until later. And with school, there have been deadlines where I cut it way too close or didn’t realize the timing until it was almost too late.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say my schedule has been formally adjusted, but I’m definitely trying to build in extra time or reminders because I’m worried about how it looks. I want to be seen as dependable, especially because I’d like to move toward an Office Manager kind of role eventually, and I worry these little missed things could make people think I’m not ready for that.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with forgetting things and concentrating something that you experience every day?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, in some form it’s pretty much every day, but it’s not like every day is a disaster or I’m forgetting major things constantly. It’s more these little everyday things that I normally would expect myself to just handle.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to return a call, and then the day gets away from me and I realize later I never did it. Or there’ll be a form I need to turn in — like a registration form or something simple — and it just sits there because I keep thinking I’ll do it in a minute, and then suddenly it’s later than I thought. Or I’ll tell myself I need to stop somewhere on the way home for an errand, and then I drive right past it or get home and remember.\n\nThat’s kind of what finally made me feel like I should ask for help. It’s not one huge thing, it’s that these little things keep slipping, and they’re things I feel like I should be able to keep track of. So yes, I’d say it shows up most days, even if the exact thing changes from day to day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two weeks or more where you felt sad or depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve definitely had days where I felt down or discouraged, especially when I’m overwhelmed or feel like I’m not keeping up, but I don’t remember a stretch of two weeks or more where I was sad or depressed nearly every day.\n\nI guess I’d say my mood can dip here and there, but it doesn’t feel like it stays that way continuously. It’s more tied to specific things happening, and then it passes. So no, not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how your friends or family interact with you since you started experiencing these difficulties with forgetting things and concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a big change, honestly. I don’t think anyone has sat me down or anything like, “You’re different,” or “What’s going on with you?” It’s more… if anything, I sometimes wonder if they’re getting a little used to reminding me about things, but I don’t know if that’s actually changed or if I’m just more aware of it now.\n\nLike with family, someone might repeat something or check, “Did you remember to do that?” but I can’t really say it’s new-new. And with friends, I don’t think they treat me differently. I haven’t had anyone pull away or seem upset in a major way. I might apologize more if I’m late responding or if I forget to follow up, but I don’t know that their interaction with me has really changed.\n\nSo… no, not in any obvious way that I can point to. It’s more my own worry that people might start seeing me as flaky, but I don’t know that they actually are.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of forgetting things and having trouble concentrating lasted for more than two years?\n\nPatient: More than two years? I don’t think I’d say it’s been noticeably like this for that long, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve probably always had moments where I’d forget something or get distracted, like anyone does, but the point where it started feeling harder to manage was when I started my evening certificate program. That was about… 15 months ago, I think. Since then it’s been much more obvious because I’m trying to keep track of work during the day and then classes and assignments and forms and schedules in the evening, and that’s when I started feeling like, okay, this is becoming a pattern I’m not keeping up with very well.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say more than two years. I’d say about a year and a few months that it’s really been noticeable and harder to handle.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
